ENS 491-492 – Graduation Project
Final Report
Project Title: Maintenance Department Management Simulation Game
Group Members: Cenker Şahin, Ege Yurtsever, Tuana Karamahmutoğlu, Arman Korkutalp, Sena Kafadar, Tolga Tektunalı
Supervisor: Gündüz Ulusoy
Date: 24.05.2026
# EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
The main objective of this project is to design and develop a multiplayer-oriented educational simulation game that simulates the management of a bus transportation system. Transportation and maintenance operations are complex decision-making processes where multiple departments, constraints, and operational factors interact on a continuous basis. Most educational tools, however, consider subjects such as scheduling, maintenance, workforce management, inventory control, and budgeting as separate entities and do not give an understanding of the interrelationships between these systems in real operational environments.
Existing enterprise software systems effectively manage industrial operations, although they are not intended for experiential learning. Similarly, many academic simulators are limited to some specific technical aspects, rather than integrated operational coordination and collaborative decision-making. Whereas this project aims to establish an interactive learning environment that allows players to engage in realistic transportation and maintenance management scenarios through multiplayer gameplay.
The system comprises interconnected managerial modules, including finance, human resources, transportation, maintenance and general management. Players observe operational indicators and make decisions under constraints such as budgets, technician availability, congestion, passenger demand and breakdown events. The project mainly employs simulation modeling, KPI-based decision systems, multiplayer gameplay mechanics, and modular system design techniques.
The project effectively established the conceptual and technical foundation for an integrated transportation management simulation environment. The expected results indicate that simulation-based collaborative gameplay can be an effective educational platform for learning concepts in industrial engineering, operational coordination, and complex decision-making processes.
# PROBLEM STATEMENT
Managing a public transportation system is a complex problem that requires the coordination of transportation and maintenance planning, human resources, finance, and strategic management. Decisions made in one department directly affect the performance of the others. 
Although Industrial Engineering courses cover topics such as simulation, queueing theory, maintenance planning, inventory control, workforce management, and financial analysis, these subjects are often taught separately. In consequence, students may have limited opportunities to understand how these concepts interact in a realistic and dynamic system. Existing enterprise software systems, such as SAP PM and IBM Maximo, are effective for managing industrial operations, but they are not designed for educational purposes. Most academic simulators also focus on specific technical areas rather than integrated decision-making.
The motivation of this project is to create an educational simulation that combines those concepts in an interactive multiplayer environment. The proposed solution is a game in which players manage an urban public bus transportation system by assuming the roles of General Manager, Maintenance Manager, Transportation Manager, Human Resources Manager, and Financial Manager. Players make decisions under constraints such as limited budgets, workforce availability, traffic congestion, uncertain passenger demand and vehicle breakdowns.
The main objective of the project is to provide a realistic learning platform where players can apply analytical methods, evaluate trade-offs between decisions, and understand how coordinated managerial decisions influence service quality, operational and financial sustainability, and the overall performance of the system.
## Objectives/Tasks
The primary goal of this project is to create and implement an educational multiplayer simulation game that simulates the operational management of an urban public bus transportation company. The project's goal is to create an interactive learning environment in which players can make realistic decisions while working within operational, financial, and resource constraints.

### Learning Objectives
As an educational simulation, the game is designed to help players develop analytical, managerial, and collaborative skills related to complete system management. After the simulation is complete and the game is played, players should be able to:
Understand how decisions in transportation, maintenance, human resources, and finance affect the overall system performance
Recognize feedback loops, bottlenecks, and dependencies among interacting departments
Interpret dashboards and KPIs to diagnose operational problems and monitor system performance
Apply Industrial Engineering application areas, such as maintenance planning, inventory control, workforce management, and financial analysis, in practice 
Evaluate trade-offs among service quality, operational reliability, cost, and employee well-being
Distinguish between short-term operational improvements and long-term strategic consequences
Collaborate and communicate effectively with other departments by submitting requests, feedback, and reports
Analyze end-of-game performance results and identify the decisions that led to success or failure
Propose alternative strategies to improve system performance in future gameplays
This project demonstrates how a game can serve as a powerful educational tool by combining analytical models with interactive decision-making. Rather than learning each concept in theory, players experience how multiple engineering and management principles operate simultaneously within a realistic system.
## Realistic Constraints
Economic Constraints
The project design and the structure of the gameplay are strongly determined by the multiplayer interaction and the real-time coordination between the many managerial roles. Under normal circumstances, such a system would usually need dedicated server infrastructure and more complex networking support. However, given the budget and resources available for the scope of the project, it is not possible to design and operate a dedicated server system.
Since the project is intended for educational  purposes, the development process relies heavily on open-source software, lightweight tools, and dependencies that can be disseminated with no major license or copyright difficulties. This constraint also affected various technical and design decisions throughout the development process, forcing the team to focus on cost-effective and scalable solutions while keeping the fundamental functionality and educational goals of the simulation system.
Technical Constraints
One of the most important technical challenges was to merge numerous interconnected systems into a single real-time simulation environment. The transportation, maintenance, finance and workforce systems are constantly exchanging information with each other, so any change in one module directly affects the others. It was a huge challenge in the development to keep the communication between these modules reliable while keeping the simulation behavior consistent.
A further major problem was the trade-off between realism and usability. While highly precise simulation logic can add realism, excessive complexity may undermine gameplay clarity and the player experience. Therefore, several systems were reduced, but the core operating logic and educational objectives of the project remained.
Moreover, the simultaneous interaction of several players with the system raised technological obstacles, such as the synchronization of multiplayer and the real-time update of KPIs.
Manufacturability Constraints
The finished output is required to be executable in various operational environments utilized by the target audience. The project is defined as an educational multiplayer simulation system, so the software structure has to be maintained accessible, stable, and practical for different conditions of use. As a result, the system architecture and gaming mechanics were created with implementation feasibility and overall system management in mind.
This constraint influenced various development decisions made during the project. In particular, the team had to balance the simulation complexity, multiplayer capabilities, and visualization features against the overall technical feasibility of the system. This resulted in some architectural simplifications in order to keep the simulation environment functional, scalable, and manageable within the project scope.
Engineering and Scientific Standards
The organizational and managerial structure of the project was conceptually designed according to ISA-95 principles of enterprise-control system integration and management of resources. The modular hierarchy utilized in the simulation is a reflection of realistic industrial organizational structures, where operational responsibilities are separated among departments such as transportation, maintenance, finance and human resources management. In addition, some performance indicators and operational decision mechanisms applied across the system were inspired by real-world transportation and maintenance management practices found in typical industrial engineering applications.
# METHODOLOGY
The methodology of the project combines simulation modelling, modular system design, KPI-based decision logic, multiplayer gameplay design, and iterative validation. The project was developed as an educational serious game rather than as a pure optimization model. Therefore, the methodology focuses on representing realistic decision situations, making system states visible to players, and ensuring that player actions produce understandable consequences.
The overall development approach followed a layered structure. First, the team defined the conceptual system and managerial roles. Second, the operational modules, along with their inputs, outputs, KPIs, and actions, were designed. Third, the simulation mechanics were connected to dashboards and decision triggers. Finally, the gameplay structure, visual layers, and testing logic were refined to improve usability and educational value.
## Conceptual System Design
At the conceptual level, the simulation is defined as a multi-agent management environment. Each player represents a managerial role inside the transportation company. The system state is represented through operational indicators, players interpret these indicators and their decisions influence the future state of the system. This creates a feedback loop between simulation outputs and managerial actions.
The five core roles are General Manager, Transportation Manager, Maintenance Manager, Human Resources Manager and Financial Manager. The General Manager monitors global KPIs and approves strategic decisions. Transportation Manager creates routes, allocates buses, and adjusts frequency. The Maintenance Manager is responsible for repairs, technicians, work orders and spare part requirements. The HR Manager controls the hiring, training, shift rotations, fatigue and morale. The Financial Manager approves purchases, tracks budgets, manages liquidity and supports procurement decisions.

## Modular Simulation Architecture
The simulation is divided into modules that correspond to functional departments. The modular structure enhances maintainability and clarifies the educational reasoning. Each module has inputs, internal state variables, outputs, KPIs and decision actions. The modules are separated to simplify the design but are interconnected by common factors like bus status, route demand, budget availability, technician availability and customer satisfaction.
The transportation module produces data on route coverage, waiting time, on-time performance, passenger demand and fleet utilization. The maintenance module produces data on bus condition, breakdown count, work-order status, MTTR, spare-part availability and technician utilization. The HR module affects technician capacity, skill level, fatigue and morale. The finance module affects procurement feasibility, budget limits, loans and vendor choices. The general management module coordinates high-level targets and approves decisions that exceed departmental authority.

## KPI-Driven Decision Framework 
The main gameplay structure is based on KPIs. KPIs are not passive values shown only for information; they are signals that guide player actions. Each KPI is defined with a purpose, measurement method, update frequency, threshold logic, and possible decision response. This helps players to understand why a problem occurs and which department should respond.
A shared system dashboard displays global indicators visible to all roles. These indicators include Customer Satisfaction Score, On-Time Performance (OTP), Average Waiting Time, Number of Transfer Trips, Failure Count,and Fleet Utilization and Cash Balance. Each role also gets its own dashboard with more specific metrics. The global dashboard enables coordination, while the role-specific dashboards enable local decision-making.

### Customer Satisfaction
Customer Satisfaction Score represents the overall passenger experience in the bus transportation system. As the passenger satisfaction is influenced by numerous operational modules simultaneously, it is included as a global KPI. Transportation decisions impact average waiting time, route connectivity, and the number of transfers required for a trip. Maintenance decisions affect the service reliability based on breakdowns and vehicle availability. Routine cleaning and labor availability also have an impact on the perceived quality of service.
Customer Satisfaction Score is calculated in the simulation as a weighted score of four main components; Waiting Time Score, Transfer Convenience Score, Breakdown Score and Cleanliness Score. The Waiting Time Score measures how long people have to wait to get on a bus. The Transfer Convenience Score measures how easily passengers can reach their destinations without making extra transfers. The Breakdown Score measures the negative impact of service disruptions caused by bus failures. The Cleanliness Score indicates the perceived quality of the bus environment during the trip.
The formula proposed is:
Customer Satisfaction Score = 0.40 × Waiting Time Score + 0.15 × Transfer Convenience Score + 0.25 × Breakdown Score + 0.20 × Cleanliness Score
Each component is scored on a scale of 0 to 100, with higher scores indicating a better passenger experience. Waiting Time Score drops as average waiting time increases. When passengers require additional transfers to finish their travels, their Transfer Convenience Score decreases. When passengers endure service interruptions due to failures, their Breakdown Score decreases. The Cleanliness Score decreases as buses are operated for extended periods of time without routine cleaning.

<w:tcPr><w:tcBorders><w:top w:color="000000" w:space="0" w:sz="6" w:val="single"/><w:left w:color="000000" w:space="0" w:sz="6" w:val="single"/><w:bottom w:color="000000" w:space="0" w:sz="6" w:val="single"/><w:right w:color="000000" w:space="0" w:sz="6" w:val="single"/></w:tcBorders></w:tcPr><w:p w:rsidR="00000000" w:rsidDel="00000000" w:rsidP="00000000" w:rsidRDefault="00000000" w:rsidRPr="00000000" w14:paraId="000000CA"><w:pPr><w:spacing w:after="0" w:line="276" w:lineRule="auto"/><w:rPr><w:sz w:val="24"/><w:szCs w:val="24"/></w:rPr></w:pPr><w:r w:rsidDel="00000000" w:rsidR="00000000" w:rsidRPr="00000000"><w:rPr><w:sz w:val="24"/><w:szCs w:val="24"/><w:rtl w:val="0"/></w:rPr><w:t xml:space="preserve">Maintenance / Routine Cleaning
This framework makes sure that the Customer Satisfaction Score is not relied on a single performance indicator. Instead, it shows the cumulative impact of route design, waiting time, service reliability, maintenance performance and bus cleanliness. Improving customer satisfaction requires collaboration among the Transportation Manager, Maintenance Manager, HR Manager, Financial Manager and General Manager.
## Transportation Modelling Method
Transportation modelling focuses on route creation, bus assignment, demand representation, schedule reliability and passenger waiting time. The simulation map is divided into residential, commercial and industrial zones. Each zone type generates different passenger-demand patterns over time. Residential areas create strong outbound demand in the morning and inbound demand in the evening. In commercial and industrial zones, there is inbound demand at the beginning of work periods and outgoing demand at the end of work periods. However, their demand patterns vary in timing and intensity. Demand peaks in industrial zones are earlier and more focused around shift start and shift finish periods . In commercial zones, work start and work end periods are about one to two hours later than in industrial zones. Furthermore, commercial zones generate more demand between the morning and evening peak periods because office, shopping, service, and other commercial activities occur throughout the day.
Demand is visualized with density layers so that the Transportation Manager can identify overloaded areas quickly. Routes passing through dense areas are displayed with stronger visual intensity. The purpose of the visualization is to create a decision-support mechanism that helps players decide where to add routes, where to assign extra buses, and which areas may be underserved.
Waiting time is measured at the stop level as the difference between passenger boarding time and passenger arrival time. Stop-level waiting time values are aggregated first at the route level and then at the system level to calculate the Global KPI: Average Waiting Time. This helps the Transportation Manager to see where the problematic stops or routes are, and the General Manager to see the overall passenger waiting experience of the whole system. On-Time Performance measures whether arrivals occur within the accepted schedule tolerance. If OTP decreases or waiting time increases, the Transportation Manager may adjust route frequency, reallocate buses, deploy spare vehicles, or request fleet expansion.
Waiting Time = Passenger Boarding Time − Passenger Arrival Time
OTP = (Number of On-Time Arrivals / Total Scheduled Arrivals) × 100
## Maintenance Modelling Method
The maintenance system models bus health, breakdown risk, preventive maintenance, routine cleaning, work orders, spare-part availability, and technician assignment. Each bus has a health indicator between 0 and 100. Health decreases with usage and breakdown events. Lower health increases the probability of breakdowns. Preventive maintenance can restore part of the health level, while reactive maintenance resolves failures and returns the bus to service.
Maintenance operations are represented through a work-order lifecycle. A work order may be created, wait for parts, wait for technicians, enter active repair, be deferred, or be completed. The distinction between waiting time and active repair time is important because it helps players diagnose whether poor performance is caused by technician capacity, spare-part shortages, or queue prioritization.
Maintenance jobs are categorized by components and complexity. Components include Engine, Body, Mechanical/Electrical, Tire, and Interior tasks. Durations are calibrated in simulation hours to preserve realistic relative magnitudes. Light jobs are short, medium jobs require partial service, and heavy jobs require substantially longer repair effort. Crew requirements depend on technician skill tiers, and higher-skilled technicians can substitute for lower-skill tasks when necessary.

Mean Time to Repair (MTTR) measures the total maintenance time from work order creation to repair completion. In the operational design, MTTR is used as an early warning signal. A rise in MTTR may suggest several operational issues such as technician fatigue, lack of available technicians, skill mismatches between the job requirements and the crew composition, queue delays, or spare part shortages. MTTR is useful to the Maintenance Manager to know whether the delay is caused by workforce capacity, workforce suitability, inventory limitations, or priority problems.
## Inventory and Vendor Logic
Spare-part availability is a key constraint because repair work cannot begin without the required parts. The project uses reorder point (ROP) logic as the main procurement trigger. Instead of relying only on a static EOQ table, the reorder point reflects lead-time demand, safety stock, and vendor reliability (the probability of a delivery delay.). Operationally, the Maintenance Manager detects the need for parts through the inventory status strip and work-order delays. Both the procurement of spare parts and vendor contracts are under the responsibility of the Financial Manager.
The Maintenance Manager generates or facilitates a purchase request when stock reaches the reorder point. The Financial Manager verifies budget availability, vendor reliability, lead time, and price. This provides a realistic connection between the maintenance operations and financial control.
The formula for ROP is (Average Part Usage × Expected Lead Time) + Safety Stock, with Average Part Usage based on recent maintenance-driven usage and Expected Lead Time adjusted by the lead-time multiplier of the selected vendor. Vendor reliability affects the required safety stock; lower reliability increases uncertainty and requires earlier ordering.

## Human Resources and Workforce Logic
The HR module supports maintenance operations by managing technician availability, skill development, fatigue, morale and the recruitment pipeline. Technician skill is represented through productivity rather than only through strict task eligibility. Level 1 technicians are the fastest and most expensive, Level 2 technicians provide moderate efficiency and cost, and Level 3 technicians are slower but less costly. This structure turns workforce assignment into a trade-off among speed, cost, and work capacity. Also, although absenteeism is an important factor we do not take it into consideration.
Fatigue is included to prevent unrealistic continuous labor use. Fatigue increases repair duration through a fatigue factor and, at high levels may make a technician unavailable for assignment. The actual duration of a maintenance job is calculated as Base Duration × Crew Efficiency Multiplier × Crew Fatigue Factor. Training improves long-term productivity, but the trained technician becomes temporarily unavailable, creating a short-term capacity loss

### Fatigue and Morale
Fatigue Points: 1 fatigue point per 2 simulation minutes (sim-minutes) of overtime. The fatigue points reset every simulation week. If a worker is on forced rest, they will have a simulation day off, and their fatigue score will reset. Also, if a bonus request is approved, morale improves and fatigue related performance pressure is partially reduced.
Morale: 100 - Fatigue Points

### Hiring Costs and Firing Costs:
The HR system tracks the financial effects of staffing decisions (hiring expenses, firing costs, wage progression and training effects). Technician skill levels range from 1 to 100. More skilled technicians are more productive and can reduce repair durations, but they also lead to higher monthly salary, hiring costs and firing costs. As a result, the HR Manager must find a balance between workforce quality and labor expenses. 
Technicians are divided into three types of candidates: flyers, classifieds and headhunters. Flyer candidates are usually lower-skilled, lower-cost technicians; classified candidates represent a  medium-level option and headhunter candidates are the higher-skilled, more expensive technicians. 
Training increases the skill level of a technician, which boosts long-term productivity, but it also increases the salary and cost level of the technician. Hiring boosts labor capacity, depending on the technician's skill level. Firing reduces future salary costs but also creates a firing cost and decreases the available workforce capacity.
## Financial and General Management Logic 
The finance system assesses whether proposed actions are affordable against a central cash balance rather than against departmental budgets. Departments request funds to purchase spare parts, hire and train staff, buy new buses, take out loans, or make scrap-related decisions. The Financial Manager will review each request based on the availability of cash, urgency, expected operational impact, and long-term financial sustainability.
The Financial Manager can approve ordinary operating expenses such as part purchases within the central cash system. General Manager approval is required for major strategic decisions like new bus purchases, loans, and scrapping, as these impact long-term capacity, cash flow, and system sustainability.
The General Manager is accountable for overall system performance to global KPIs and is the ultimate decision maker for strategic actions that affect long term capacity, financial risk, system-wide service quality or fare policy. The choices include fleet expansion, loan approval, bus scrapping, high impact resource allocation decisions that cannot be handled within a single department, route based ticket pricing and second transfer discount rules. Depending on demand, service quality, and budget, ticket costs can be changed at the route level. The General Manager manages a strategic trade-off between passenger satisfaction and financial sustainability.

## Interface, Visualization, and Notification Design
The interface is designed to support fast interpretation of operational conditions. A global System Status Indicator is visible to all players and can show Normal, Warning or Critical states. The indicator functions like a traffic light: green means the system is stable, yellow means a delay or overload is emerging, and red means that an urgent disruption such as a breakdown or route segment without assigned buses requires immediate action.
## Dashboard and Role-Specific Information Design 
One important methodological decision was to distinguish between global metrics and role-specific metrics. The global dashboard and the overall health of the system are used by all players. Since its role is coordination, it does not show too many details of operations. In contrast, role dashboards have the detailed indicators needed for local decisions. It separates out information to prevent overload and allows communication between players.
Role-specific dashboards show only the information needed for that role's decisions. This reduces cognitive load and prevents players from being overwhelmed by unrelated information. Supporting details remain available through tooltips, drill-down views, maps, and queues. Notifications are used when one role's action changes another role's decision environment. For example, if the Transportation Manager flags a bus for frequent delays or risk of a breakdown, the Maintenance Manager gets a notification and can prioritize that bus in the queue for maintenance. Once the bus is repaired, the Transportation Manager is notified so that it can be transferred back to active service.
For the Transportation Manager, the dashboard emphasizes service access and reliability. Stop Coverage shows whether the network reaches the intended areas. On-Time Performance shows whether routes are operating according to schedule. Waiting time is available through route or stop-level details, especially through map tooltips, so the player can investigate local congestion without making the summary dashboard too crowded.
For the Maintenance Manager, the dashboard emphasizes fleet readiness and repair capacity. The work-order queue shows current maintenance load; the technician status board shows skill, availability, and fatigue of the workforce; the fleet condition overview helps identify buses near failure; and the inventory strip identifies parts that may block repairs. This layout directly supports the player's main task: keeping enough buses available for transportation while controlling long-term reliability.
HR and Finance dashboards are meant to be support dashboards and not direct operational control panels. HR covers technician workload, crew productivity, fatigue, morale, training, and the recruitment pipeline. Finance is concerned with the current cash balance, weekly net profit, weekly cash burn rate, pending funding requests, vendor options, and loan status. 
The General Manager views global indicators in summary form, unresolved cross-departmental concerns, safety-buffer settings, frequent system alarms, and strategic decision requests.

# RESULTS & DISCUSSION
The Maintenance Department Management Simulation Game has successfully moved from a conceptual framework to a functional simulation environment. The project has largely realized its primary objective of creating an interactive learning platform that integrates multiple industrial engineering disciplines.
## Realization of Initial Objectives and Tasks
The extent to which the eight original tasks have been realized is detailed below:
Task 1: Game Architecture Design
Five Player Command Center: The game is centered around a shared dashboard in which five distinct managers, which are General Manager, Transportation Manager, Maintenance Manager, HR Manager, and Financial Manager, collaborate to run the bus company.
Host Referee System: One player’s computer will be like the central host, running the main simulation rules and clock. The other players will view live updates and send their management decisions back to the designated player.
Inter-Department Workflows: An action that a player takes will affect the other players as well. For instance, when the Financial Manager approves a parts order, the vendor system is notified to process and ship the delivery. Once the shipment arrives, the Maintenance Manager is also notified and the delivered parts are added to the maintenance inventory for repair operations. 
Task 2: Functional Module Development
Maintenance: Simulates realistic wear and tear as buses drive. If a part degrades too much, the bus breaks down and generates a repair ticket. The player selects the crew, which will make the repair.
HR & Finance: HR manages hiring, training, and assigning staff to specific depots or teams to boost local efficiency. Finance tracks the company ledger, handling part purchases, payroll, operational costs, revenues, and penalty fees. Also, it selects the vendors.
Transportation: At the start, the player chooses the bus routes. Also governs bus schedules, routes, and passenger demand, ensuring buses cover the city map.
Task 3: Adding Multiplayer Support
Live Synchronization: Makes sure that every action taken by a single player immediately updates the game of all connected players. This keeps the team on the same page.
Seamless Joining: When a player connects to the game mid-session, the game instantly packages up the current state of the company, including current stock, active contracts, and repair queues. This makes it so that they can jump right into managing without missing context.
Task 4: Performance Evaluation System
Efficiency Metrics: The game automatically tracks key business indicators, such as Mean Time to Repair (MTTR), which measures how long a bus waits in a broken state before returning to service, and On-Time Performance (OTP) for route schedule reliability.
Workload Dashboards: Visual gauges compare available labor with pending repairs. This provides the players with color-coded warnings when a depot is running smoothly, or at full capacity, or severely understaffed.
Task 5: Scenario Generation and Calibration
Consistent Randomness: Uses mathematical formulas to generate random events such as part durability and delivery speed so the outcomes are synchronized in all players' screens.
Supply Chain Risks: Shipments can be delayed based on a vendor's reliability rating, forcing players to adapt to sudden part shortages. Also, parts with high friction wear out faster than standard frames, which reward proactive stockpiling.
Traffic Congestion Events: Sudden congestion increases travel times and delays bus schedules, forcing Transportation Managers to adapt route planning.
Demand Fluctuation Scenarios: Passenger demand changes dynamically depending on time of day or random city events, creating peak-hour pressure on fleet operations.
Unexpected Vehicle Failures: Rare but severe breakdowns can suddenly remove buses from service and increase maintenance workload.
Workforce Availability Issues: Technician or driver shortages caused by fatigue or insufficient staffing reduce operational efficiency.
Financial Stress Conditions: Rising operational costs or unexpected penalty fees create budget pressure and force departments to prioritize spending decisions.
Weather-Related Operational Effects (planned/optional): Adverse weather conditions may increase breakdown probability and reduce transportation efficiency.
Task 6: User Experience and Interface Design
Custom Responsive Layouts: Rather than automatic stretching, the system utilizes interface panels and tailored boundaries, which ensure complex side-by-side management dashboards stay clean and organized.
Smooth Performance: Lists with fast updating attributes like active buses or repair queues reuse existing interface cards instead of constantly deleting and recreating the lists, which keeps the game running smoothly without any visual stutter.
Intuitive Controls:Drag-and-drop tools allow managers to reorder repair queues, maintenance tasks, and operational priorities efficiently Clear labels show exact needs like "Winter Tire" instead of just "Tires" alongside simple progress bars, allowing players to spot bottlenecks at a glance.
Task 7: Testing and Validation
Multiplayer Stability: Testing makes sure the main simulation doesn't suddenly stop or drop inputs when multiple players make decisions at the same time.
Error Prevention: Background checks guarantee that rapid UI interaction, like fast drag and drop actions, don't transmit missing data within the network, keeping the game stable and crash-free.
Task 8: Documentation Development
Standard Operating Procedures: Fully documents the management loops, rules, and specific responsibilities tied to each of the five player roles.
Game Math and Balancing: Compiles all the underlying math for business scoring, employee skill progression, and financial tracking into a clear design reference.
## End of Game Role-Based Performance Evaluation
As the game concludes, the system generates a role-based evaluation report and an overall team performance summary. The team overview is based on global metrics such as Customer Satisfaction Score, On-Time Performance, Average Waiting Time, Number of Transfer Trips, Failure Count, Fleet Utilization and Cash Balance. The report is then role based to highlight the contribution of each managerial job to the final outcome.
The goal of this evaluation is not just to determine whether the company performed well, but also to help players understand how their local decisions influenced system-wide results.

This end of game review encourages reflective learning by connecting decisions, KPI outcomes, and interdepartmental coordination to the final simulation result.
## Differences From the Initial Objectives
In the architectural design of the game, there were major changes. The original design of the game centered on 12-period monthly simulation cycles; the realized version of the project uses a State-Event-Action feedback loop that prioritizes real-time synchronization between departments. For the functional module, the major change was in the budget part. In the initial phase, there were separate budgets for each department, but in the realized project, there is an integrated cash system in which the Financial Manager approves individual funding requests like buying equipment. Moreover, the skill system for technicians was further developed to include fatigue impact and crew productivity models. Also, to stay within the budget and resource constraints the realized project uses Unity Netcode for GameObjects (NGO) co-op structure instead of the dedicated server infrastructure for multiplayer support. When it comes to performance evaluation and scenarios, the system uses an engine, which dynamically handles city growth and has modifiers, which are temporary, like sports events or extreme weather conditions, rather than static scripts. While the backend data system for KPIs is fully realized, the implementation of advanced 3D geographic overlays and specialized breakdown indicators differs significantly from the original visual targets, as functionality was prioritized over aesthetics. User interface and testing priorities also shifted towards functional clarity and short-term synchronization stability, rather than aesthetics or long-term cycle validation.
## Contribution to State-of-the-Art
The project provides an integrated role-based management environment that adds to educational simulation tools rather than on a particular operational function. Unlike enterprise systems like SAP PM and IBM Maximo, which are primarily designed to support real-world industrial operations, this game is intended for experiential learning. It helps players to understand how transportation planning, maintenance operations, workforce planning, inventory and vendor decisions, financial control, KPI-based monitoring and strategic approvals work together inside the same bus transportation system. In this sense, the game encourages learning not just through individual actions, but also through collaboration among departments and tradeoffs at the system level.
## Carbon Footprint
Since the game employs a lightweight simulation structure and multiplayer support rather than continuous cloud hosting, its direct digital carbon footprint is predicted to be low. The project does not necessitate physical production or extensive server infrastructure. Furthermore, the game promotes sustainability concepts by encouraging preventive maintenance, effective fleet utilization and longer vehicle lifecycles.
## Economic Feasibility
The project is well-suited for educational adoption due to its use of open-source dependencies and the absence of Freedom to Use (FTU) issues. The logic of the game supports circularity by challenging players to optimize the lifespan of their current fleet through preventive maintenance, thereby decreasing the need for premature capital expenditure.
# IMPACT
The project has educational, technological, and socio-economic implications by establishing an interactive environment, where players can experience real operational decision-making inside a public bus transportation system. This project brings together transportation management, maintenance operations, financial planning, and workforce management in the Maintenance Department in an integrated simulation environment. This helps users better comprehend the impact of actions made in one department on overall system performance.
From a technical perspective, the project combines simulation modeling, KPI-based decision systems, multiplayer coordination mechanics, and dynamic visualization tools in one platform. The system constantly produces operational data such as passenger demand, fleet condition, and breakdown cases that are converted into managerial indicators in real time, which guide player decisions.
The project also has special features in terms of gameplay and educational framework. The system creates dynamic and constantly changing operational settings, such as delayed spare-part deliveries, workforce shortages, and budget pressure scenarios etc., rather than static scenarios  where players have to collaborate with each other and react to emerging challenges. It turns the simulation into a shared decision-support and learning environment. In the future, the project may also have the potential to be commercialized as an educational serious game that may be utilized in industrial engineering courses, training programs or simulation-based learning environments.
The development procedure does not present any significant Freedom-to-Use (FTU) difficulties. The project is mostly based on original system designs and open-source development tools.
In terms of sustainability, the initiative promotes optimal resource use, efficient fleet utilization, and operational efficiency. In the game, the players are challenged to avoid unnecessary breakdowns, better use of the vehicles and manage operational resources more efficiently. More efficient repair planning and the reduction of avoidable service disruptions boost sustainable transportation operations, which helps to eliminate unnecessary operational waste.
The project also supports sustainable engineering by putting long-term asset management ahead of short-term operational considerations. Players should be encouraged to use their existing fleet resources wisely and only seek new ones when necessary, encouraging real world sustainability and resource efficiency principles.
# ETHICAL ISSUES
This project identified no serious ethical or legal difficulties. The proposed system is an educational simulation game that contains no dangerous products, surveillance technologies, or applications that might harm human health or personal privacy. Furthermore, no patent-protected technologies or copyrighted systems were directly utilized during development. As a result, no significant ethical concerns about the project's execution or use are expected.
# PROJECT MANAGEMENT
At the beginning of the project, the main focus was on building a maintenance-oriented simulation system. However, the project grew in scale as it progressed, becoming a larger multiplayer transportation management simulation with several interconnected management departments. This evolution greatly increased the project's educational value and technical complexity.
One of the biggest problems in managing the project was the need to coordinate the development of multiple interlinked systems at the same time. Usually, the simulation engine, the gaming mechanics, and the user interface components are developed at different speeds, which causes synchronization and integration problems in the development. In particular, making the simulation outputs consistent with the gameplay-oriented decision structures needed frequent revisions and cooperation among the team members.
Another significant challenge was to find a balance between realism and usability. Our objective was to create a very realistic operational environment, but if we included all the real-world details into the game, the system would be too complex and difficult for players to operate. Therefore, many systems were simplified, but the basic operating logic and the educational purpose of the project were preserved.
During development, we revised the project plan numerous times as new technical challenges and gameplay requirements surfaced. As the project grew from maintenance simulation to a comprehensive transportation management system, we also revisited some design decisions and development goals. To keep the project running well, we maintained constant communication among team members and the Instructor and coordinated the development of the different modules.
Overall, this project gave us practical experience in working with a complicated interconnected system. We improved our understanding of system integration, the importance of teamwork, and project management while also learning how to balance realistic simulation mechanics with gameplay simplicity and usability.
# CONCLUSION AND FUTURE WORK
This project resulted in the creation and implementation of an educational multiplayer simulation game that simulates the operational management of a bus transport company. Initially, the project was oriented towards maintenance management, but the scope developed into a more general transportation management simulation that combines maintenance, transportation, human resources, finance, and general management. The project's growth enhanced its educational value by enabling participants to observe how decisions made in one area affected the performance and limitations of the others.
The most significant outcome of the project is the development of an integrated decision-making environment based on simulation outputs, KPI-based dashboards, role-based actions, and cross-departmental workflows. Before making decisions, players have to understand system indications such as customer satisfaction, on-time performance, average waiting time, failure count, available buses, technician usage, and cash balance. Therefore, the game provides an experiential learning environment that integrates theoretical concepts of Industrial Engineering such as maintenance planning, workforce management, inventory control, financial decision making, and system coordination into interactive gameplay.
The project also showed that a simulation game can function as a decision support and learning environment and not just as a technical model. The role-based structure encourages player collaboration, while the central cash system, vendor selection logic, reorder point-based purchasing, fatigue effects, training tradeoffs, and cross-department coordination procedures provide realistic managerial restrictions. These factors assist players to recognize that operational performance cannot be enhanced by isolated decisions alone. Sustainable performance involves the coordination of departments and a careful balancing of service quality, cost, labor capacity, and long-term asset management.
Future work will be aimed at enhancing the integration and calibration of all modules. The first step should be to check the KPI thresholds, event probabilities, vendor parameters, fatigue effects and training impacts by multiple simulation runs. Second, the user interface can be improved by providing better dashboards, visual feedback and more detailed map-based indicators for demand, congestion, breakdowns and fleet condition. Third, the performance evaluation framework can further be extended to provide more granular post-game comments for particular departments and overall team performance. This will enable players to know the decisions that lead to success or failure and help in reflective learning after the game play.
To make the game more sustainable and cost-effective, later versions may include more detailed environmental indicators, such as how much fuel is used, how much energy is used, and how decisions to add more vehicles or get rid of some could affect the environment. This would allow the simulation to teach not only operational and financial sustainability, but also environmental responsibility. Procurement and asset management systems should also be enhanced to encourage reuse, preventive maintenance and extended vehicle lifecycles.
In summary, the project laid a solid conceptual and functional groundwork for an educational transportation management simulation. Additional testing, tuning, interface improvements and sustainability expansions can turn the system into a valuable serious game for Industrial Engineering education and simulation-based management training.
# APPENDIX
The following section contains supplementary screenshots and descriptive figures from the game’s management interface. These visuals illustrate the various management panels, user interface designs, and core gameplay mechanics discussed throughout the document.
Fig. 1: Overview of various transit routes designed by the Transportation Manager. This panel showcases the fleet's operational paths and route coverage across the map.
Fig. 2: Comprehensive fleet overview illustrating overall health scores, individual part conditions, and current bus statuses. Color indicators vary based on the calculated health metrics of each vehicle.
Fig. 3: Finance Manager panel for part procurement and vendor contracting. The interface displays parts inventory, and delivery schedules.
Fig. 4 : Maintenance Manager’s control panel featuring threshold configuration and workflow optimization. The interface includes a draggable priority queue for part repairs, real-time worker workload monitoring, and a live maintenance queue with detailed diagnostic access.
Fig. 5: Shared Inventory Management panel for Maintenance and Finance Managers. The interface tracks current stock levels across all components, utilizing color-coded alerts to indicate critical shortages or low-inventory thresholds.
Fig. 6: Human Resources Panel for managing active staff and hiring new employees. The interface includes options to train or fire active mechanics and 3 different options to find new candidates.
Fig. 7: The panel maintenance manager uses to make requests of the HR manager. Hiring or training can be requested. A similar panel is used to make requests of the Financial Manager.
Fig. 8: A visual representation of how the incoming request is observed by the HR manager. The progress is tracked and once the request is completed or it is rejected the message disappears.
Fig. 9: Financial Manager panel to track transactions and request loans. The left side provides a singleton view of most current transactions in a scroll view. The right side includes graphs that visualize different types of transactions and a section to make loan requests of the general manager.
Fig. 10: The General Manager panel that includes a summary of general statistics and controls to change ticket prices and discount rates on transit travels.
Fig. 11: A visualization of traffic.
Fig. 12: A visualization of in demand.
Fig. 13: A visualization of out demand.
Fig. 14: An example of a general report that evaluates the team performance. Each player is presented with this general report at the end of the game.
Fig. 15: An example of a player based performance report. Each player is presented with one report specialized for their role at the end of the game.
